ParlaMint-DK_20160426130623	The debate is open. Minister of Education and Research. We need to calm down on the way out of the Chamber, because we are now in the process of negotiating.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426130639	The motion for a resolution aims to allow universities to take over the state-owned buildings that they are currently renting against payment of a share of the property valuation. This means that the universities currently covered by the SEA scheme must be given the opportunity to switch to building property. However, this possibility already exists. Today, the universities have an opportunity to apply for building-related ownership as set out in Section 30 of the University Act. The University Act also describes a process and framework conditions for any transfer. The government doesn't see any reason to change those rules. The current rules include, inter alia, the possibility of an individual assessment of a possible transfer, which must, as a general rule, be cost-neutral for the State. The motion for a resolution envisages a more automatic transitional option, and the proposal to set the price for a share of the property assessment also provides for a discount for universities in connection with the transfer of buildings. The Government wishes to maintain that, in the case of the transfer of buildings, there should be a concrete assessment. The Government cannot agree to an automatic system which does not take due financial account of the specific conditions relating to the university and the building stock which may be involved. Secondly, the government considers that the time has not come to grant substantial discounts in connection with any transfer of buildings. The principle of cost neutrality should therefore be maintained. In the comments, the proposal is also justified by a criticism of the existing management of buildings, i.e. the SEA scheme. To this I would like to point out that last autumn the government adopted a reform of the SEA regime. Danish universities' feedback on the reform has overall been positive. I take this as an expression of the fact that the reform meets a number of the wishes raised by universities. With the reform, universities will be given priority over the renewal and maintenance of buildings and laboratories, which will allow ongoing reinvestment, which will not mean increased rent for universities. The Ministry of Transport and Building has entered into dialogue with universities on the implementation of the reform. Now, in motion for a resolution B 85, the Liberal Alliance has not specifically referred to the University of Copenhagen's own request, but I would like to preface any questions with the following comments: a university, namely the University of Copenhagen, has been dealt with with an application for building autonomy according to the current rules of the University Law. As most people here know, the university has been refused its request. This has been done by reference to an overall economic assessment that for the State as a whole the most appropriate management and management of state-owned educational buildings is achieved by maintaining central management of these buildings. The government has thus made a concrete balance of advantages and disadvantages for the University of Copenhagen and the State as a whole. The Government cannot therefore support the proposal, since the possibility of seeking and processing a request for building ownership has already been included in the university law. The Government does not wish to introduce an automatic transitional option, as proposed in B 85, but wishes to maintain the principle of a specific position on a case-by-case basis.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426131026	Since no one has asked for short remarks, we thank the Minister and go further in the rapporteur's line. This is Mrs Mette Reissmann, the Social Democratic Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426131043	Motion for a resolution B 85 on building property lies in the wake of a discussion which has been going on for many years now. However, the discussion is still relevant, because there are, of course, a number of advantages for universities if they achieve building-related ownership. I think most people in the debate will recognize that. The arguments are very much based on whether universities will then be able to make more long-term and therefore better economic priorities if they own the buildings and thus lend them. Similarly, if the authorities are not to be involved to the same degree as is the case today, some bureaucracy will certainly be removed in the ongoing renovation. The Socialists, of course, listen when universities say that this issue is of great importance to their ability to undertake their research and teaching efforts. We are also prepared to discuss the question of ownership thoroughly. The Socialists believe that such a discussion should take place between the parties, and this must be done on a proper and very enlightened basis. I have to say that the motion for a resolution tabled by the Liberal Alliance does not respond to that wish in this case. It would be irresponsible to take a decision against this background, since the resolution does not provide any calculations on the consequences in either the short or the long term. That is why I believe that the discussion, if it is to be taken, must be taken under the authority of the Ministry. But there are, of course, both economic and training- and research-related consequences of these situations. In that case, I would also expect that a discussion could, of course, be held on a sufficiently enlightened basis. But it is the government that has the ball on this issue and the Socialists do not support the resolution. However, I would like to add one comment in relation to the motivation behind the proposal by the proposers. One can of course read in the comments to the motion for a resolution that the aim is to ensure that universities can spend more money on research and education, as well as to support universities' long-term economic priorities. These are all positive intentions, I think, but I would just like to remind you that the proposers, together with the rest of the blue bloc, are behind a financial law that swings the green harvester over universities, with the result that billions are being cut on research and education. This does not create better opportunities to give long-term priority, and that, of course, also means cuts in research and education, with great consequences for quality. So this is a call to start with its own billion savings on education and research when you present your otherwise good intentions to the Folketing.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426135056	Thank you for that, and thank you to the SF for the motion for a resolution to set up an independent institution in the field of alternative treatment to ensure research and dissemination of knowledge on alternative treatment and natural medicine. The reason for the proposal is that an increasing number of Danes are using alternative treatment. From this point of view, the SF's proposal to focus more on alternative treatment is quite fine, and it is an intention that we in the government fully share. However, although I also fully share the intention behind the SF's proposal, I am at the same time a little puzzled by the resolution here today, because if you turn the time back just a little bit, it was precisely with a health minister from the SF in the lead that the then S-R-SF government from January 2013 chose to close down the then ViFAB, the Knowledge and Research Centre for Alternative Treatment. ViFAB was precisely an independent institution aimed at ensuring research into and disseminating knowledge on alternative treatment and natural medicine. But now you can understand that the SF seems to have changed its mind and wants this government to create a new form of ViFAB. Perhaps it is a little strange in itself that the SF is asking a new government to give priority to alternative treatment, after you only cut down on it yourself. Having said that, the SF may also be a little late because, as I think it is also SF known, the government, together with the other parties behind the Finance Act, the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservatives, has already strengthened the area of alternative treatment. We have set aside 5 million in the Finance Act. kr. of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment; of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment; of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment; of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment; of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment; of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment, out of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment, out of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment, out of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment, out of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment, out of the 5 million ECU for alternative treatment, out of the 5 million ECU for The pool focuses on bridge-building between the treatment in the established healthcare system and alternative treatment methods, which is why it can be sought by patient associations that enter into partnerships with research institutions, established health services, trade associations for alternative practitioners, RAB-registered practitioners or others. This in itself contrasts with the previous government, which, as I said, with a minister of health from the SF, removed the funds allocated to research into alternative treatment. The current government gives priority to refocusing on research in alternative treatment. We have done this with the 2016 Finance Act and, in addition to research, the remaining part of the 5 million will. kr., i.e. 1 million. kr., be used for more information and communication about alternative treatment. In 2016, the Board of Patient Safety, which serves the Council for Alternative Treatment, holds a series of conferences, workshops and information meetings for both alternative practitioners and authorised healthcare professionals to raise awareness across the board. The Council's website will also be upgraded with a number of professional contributions on alternative treatment, and citizens will be able to go to the Council's website and search for registered alternative practitioners near their place of residence, and at the same time, through leaflets and on social media, will inform citizens about, among other things: The RAB scheme and means of redress. Overall, it is my expectation that the 5 million will be spent. kr. will be able to achieve increased research-based knowledge on alternative treatment, and at the same time we will support bridge building and partnerships between the established healthcare system and the research environment, patient associations and alternative practitioners, and that is what is needed. Finally, the funds will also help to raise awareness between alternative practitioners and authorised health professionals and to improve the dress of the population in relation to their opportunities and rights when using alternative treatment. Then, in the motion for a resolution, the proposers mention that an independent alternative treatment institution can help to strengthen patients' safety when using alternative treatment methods. When you read the motion for a resolution, you can almost get the impression that it is a little uncertain for patients today to take advantage of alternative treatment options. I do not think I quite share the view expressed in the motion for a resolution, but that is perhaps not quite the case with the SF, because the safety of patients is already being dealt with within the current framework. As the whole parliament is aware, we have the so-called RAB system, which is an industry-managed registration system, where the purpose is precisely to strengthen the safety of Danes when they use alternative treatment. When the patient chooses a RAB practitioner, he or she can be sure that the treatment provider meets a number of requirements including: training, ethics and good clinical practice, and at the same time the patient, the citizen, has the right to complain about a RAB practitioner of the association that has registered the practitioner. And even though the government has now set aside 5 million. kr. In the case of alternative treatment, that does not mean that nothing is happening today in relation to alternative treatment. The Danish Health Authority states that alternative treatment is part of the Danish Agency's professional work on national clinical guidelines. So far, alternative treatment is included in five clinical guidelines, including: with focus on acupuncture, climate therapy and mindfulness. In addition, we also have the Danish Health Authority's Board for Alternative Treatment, which performs a central role in providing knowledge about alternative treatment to citizens, patients and health professionals. At the same time, the Council is the main central cooperation forum between alternative practitioners and the relevant authorities. It also involves the important task of providing a bridge between alternative treatment and the more established health system; a bridge building which the government considers to be central to strengthening alternative treatment for the benefit of patients. Finally, there is the economic part of SF's proposal. Here the SF is proposing that the 5 million already earmarked for the 2016 Finance Act should be transferred to the independent institution you want to set up as a start. It has to be said that, in 2016, the funds have already been used, even though the SF is now proposing to spend money that has not been taken responsibility for helping to find it. At the same time, SF forgets to mention or point out from which to get the money to run the independent institution after 2016. So, all in all, we have to understand that the proposal is unfunded. Finally, let me give a brief summary: in the government we acknowledge the intention of the SF's proposal to strengthen the area of alternative treatment. That is also why we have prioritised funding for the area through the Finance Act for 2016. At the same time, we consider that the safety of patients in relation to alternative treatment is already adequately maintained today; this is done, among other things. through the RAB scheme and through the work in general in the Danish Agency for Patient Safety and the Danish Health Authority. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

The Danish Health Authority is the top health professional authority in Denmark, and a collection of all functions related to alternative treatment will, in my opinion, help to ensure that there is a bridge between alternative practitioners and health professionals to a greater extent than today. It is therefore surprising that SF has now turned on a plate and sees the need for an independent institution when SF's former Minister for Health and Prevention was himself in charge of dismantling ViFAB a few years ago. Last but not least, I must not fail to mention that the proposal is unfunded, because although the comments point to funds from the Finance Act for 2016, no funds have been identified for the operation of an independent institution after 2016. And if I don't remember quite wrong, I think it was also the former Minister for Health and Prevention, Astrid Krag of the SF, who said that she didn't have access to a money tree in the backyard. As the resolution is unfunded, I must conclude that this is still the case for the SF. The left cannot support the motion for a resolution with the comments that have been made.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426141729	Thank you to the rapporteur. We shall move on to the rapporteur's report. Peder Hvelplund, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426141739	It is of course a fact that there is a major discrepancy between interest in alternative treatment and the Danish state's investment in development and research in alternative treatment. The great popular interest in alternative treatment requires active participation by the state, and we in the Enhedslisten would like to support this. As several people have mentioned earlier, ViFAB, the Knowledge and Research Centre for Alternative Treatment, was established in order to be able to raise the area and optimise both knowledge and research in different alternative treatment types. The previous government chose to close the Centre in 2012 and hand over the task to the Danish Health Authority, but without the same funding, and there we have unfortunately seen that in practice this has meant a heavy under-priority of the area. Now there are a number of people who have expressed astonishment at the fact that the SF is putting forward this particular proposal. I would like to say that we are not at all puzzled. We think it is very, very wise of the SF to make this proposal, because it was a wrong decision that was made at the time, and we can then hope that the same wisdom will affect the rest of the Folketing. The creation of a new and independent centre, which this proposal proposes, will provide a stronger focus on alternative treatment options in the major public diseases and on the most widely used alternative therapies. I also think it is important to emphasise that the government's proposal is just an infinite amount of effort in relation to public and, in particular, private investment in traditional medical treatment. These are investments and treatment approaches, which are very much dictated by the pharmaceutical industry, which, of course, have very important interests at stake in maintaining the status quo. In Enhedslisten we acknowledge that there are a great many Danes who choose alternative solutions and approaches each year rather than traditional medicine, and therefore we would like to support research and especially knowledge gathering on alternatives to biomedical treatments, which often have quite major side effects both human and economic. At the same time, we support the proposal's idea of an independent centre, which ensures independence from the interests of the pharmaceutical industry and from the general and traditional biomedical approach, which may naturally be influenced by the Danish Health Authority. Throughout history, the medical world has often rejected alternative treatment in strong terms. There are huge economic and research interests at stake, and we know that the pharmaceutical industry has had an overly decisive influence on both research and the choice of therapies by doctors. However, more and more doctors and researchers recognise and refer to alternative practitioners, and we see that there will be increased recognition of the alternative sector as positive. We would like to help push forward this positive development by supporting this proposal. In the Enhedslisten we also note that access to alternative treatment is far away reserved for people with financial resources, since alternative treatment often takes place privately and is quite expensive. This is therefore an inequality in health for many of the groups that are being cut off from treatment. The Enhedslisten would therefore also like to emphasise that we support the proposal in order to document the impact of alternative treatment and thus to incorporate new and effective methods into public health care. An important part of the proposal also envisages the creation of evidence as to which alternative therapies actually have an effect. Unfortunately, we have seen too many examples of attempts to incorporate alternative treatment into the public sector, which has been shut down in recent years due to savings. One area in which we have seen good results is, among other things, one of the areas where we have seen good results. use of acupuncture during pregnancy and childbirth and in some places during chemotherapy. Such initiatives with free, effective and side-effect-free treatment are much more widespread, and it is actually a mystery to us that research in this area will not be given priority. It is good that 5 million will be set aside. kr. in the Finance Act here, but, as I said earlier, it is infinitely small in relation to what else is being devoted to research. At the same time, we believe that it is important to support the intention contained in the proposal to create an independent institution in which efforts can be concentrated in research and evidence. This is what this resolution does, and that is why the Enhedslisten supports the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426143530	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Employment.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426143614	Thank you. I would like to start from behind with B 172, which I would like to thank Enhedslisten for presenting. To that extent, I could reject the proposal that it is a blow in the air, because it is totally unrealistic to have the necessary support for it among the other EU countries, and in our own view the time is not available to discuss treaty changes. But I'm not gonna do that. I would like to discuss the substance of the proposal, because I think there is reason to correct some misunderstandings. In particular, the purpose of a social protocol must be to ensure that social rights always take precedence in cases of opposing economic rights. The comments on the motion for a resolution state that workers' rights and the right of Member States to protect themselves against social dumping are not guaranteed by EU rules. It goes on to say that the European Court of Justice has prohibited trade unions and Member States from guaranteeing posted workers the same pay as nationals of host countries by law or collective agreements. These are some grounds for the motion for a resolution which, in my view, are wrong. The EU and the European Court of Justice recognise workers' rights and their enforcement. There are several examples of this which the proposers themselves are aware of, because one of them is directly mentioned in the comments to B 171. This is the case between a Finnish trade union and a Polish company. In its decision, the European Court of Justice emphasises that the wording of the Posting of Workers Directive means that the minimum wage is defined in accordance with the national law and/or practice of the Member State in whose territory the worker is posted. The judgment also states that the minimum wage for posted workers may not only be the free choice of the posted employer for the sole purpose of offering lower wage costs than local labour costs. I believe this is an expression of the fact that Member States have the opportunity and the right to ensure orderly working conditions in the labour market. What is crucial for a competitive and socially sustainable European Union is that freedom of movement and social rights are balanced against each other. Consideration of the service provider and freedom of movement implies that a service provider must be able to calculate in advance the cost of a task, including what a specific worker is entitled to according to the nature of the work, qualifications, seniority, etc. It is therefore impossible to wait until the service provider is aware of the costs involved in the subsequent agreement at local level to determine the actual salary. With the Danish Laval solution together with the social partners, we have found a good solution for this. It is a balanced solution to a specific problem which supports the work of the social partners on orderly conditions. I could also mention another important tool that supports orderly relationships, and that is our RUT register. The European Court of Justice has not expressed an opinion on this, but has dealt with the similar Belgian system, LIMOSA. Here the Court expressly states that the protection of workers through the fight against social dumping is one of the concerns which can be taken into account even if it interferes with freedom of movement. The government sees this as a balanced approach between the interests of free movement and the interests of workers' rights, and we think that is the right balance. It is clear that if you are against the internal market and against the European Union, you can also disagree with the fact that there must be a balance between the rights of workers. This is an honest matter, but the government is in favour of the internal market, of which both companies and workers have great advantages. It is always possible to discuss how the actual balance should be made and how we should combat cheating and abuse in practice, but we can do so within the existing Treaty framework, and that is one of the things we can do. The European Commission's proposal for a revision of the Posting of Workers Directive expresses. I will come back to that in a moment when I will explain the government's position on B 171. So, in the opinion of the government, a social protocol is not necessary and, as I said at the outset, it is not realistic to come through. The government wants to pursue its objectives using realistic means. With these words, I must inform you that the government cannot support the B 172. Now let me get back to B 171. The proposal has both a substantive aspect and a procedural aspect, namely the procedures for obtaining the government's mandate for negotiations. I shall start with the substance, and it is clear from my comments on the proposal for a social protocol that the government basically has a different view of the balance between free movement and workers' rights than the proposers have. At the same time, however, the government believes that there is more scope for protecting the interests of workers in current EU law than the proposers suggest. There is a central part of the legislation being made up precisely of the Posting of Workers Directive. The Posting of Workers Directive reflects the balance I have talked about. The Directive, on the one hand, allows Member States to enforce the most central terms and conditions of employment with regard to posted workers, and, on the other hand, provides seconded undertakings with knowledge of what to conform to. The first Finland judgment confirms that Member States can use a broad minimum wage concept. The Government's immediate interpretation is that the Commission's proposal for a revision of the Posting of Workers Directive consolidates the interpretation of minimum wages by the European Court of Justice following the current directive, and we will of course clarify this in the negotiations, and we will ensure that the definition of pay and other conditions remains a national matter, and on this point I fully agree with the proposers. This brings me to the procedural aspect of the resolution. In accordance with the usual procedures agreed between the Government and the Parliamentary Committee on Europe, the Government has sent a basic and proximity note to the Committee. As usual, I will inform the committee of the work on the presentation of Council agendas and, at an appropriate time, present the matter for discussion. With the proposal, the proposers propose a procedure for obtaining a mandate other than that agreed between the Government and the Folketing. It's a bad thing, and I can't recommend it. With these words, I must inform you that the government cannot support B 171 either. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426145402	Then we thank the Minister and move on to the rapporteur's line. The first is sir. Peter Hummelgaard Thomsen, Social Democrats.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426145425	Thank you for the floor, President. I shall start with, because that is what I have the most to say, Amendment B 171. First of all, I would like to thank the authors of the motion for a resolution for bringing the debate on this subject, in our opinion, very crucially on the agenda in this Chamber. The Commission's work programme for 2016 set the objective of implementing a principle of the same pay for the same job in the same place. Implementation of this principle would be a unique step in the fight against social dumping at European level, and the announcement was originally that the Commission would present a revised Posting of Workers Directive which could implement the principle in full. At the same time, the directive should respect national labour market models and should not affect national wage formation. In the Social Democratic Party, we welcome the fact that the European Commission has taken steps to reopen the Posting of Workers Directive on the basis of an objective of ensuring equal pay for equal work. The measure has taken place under pressure from a large number of countries, including Denmark, which is concerned with adapting EU legislation in a way that will enable us to combat social dumping and brain drain, etc. In particular, the revised provisions of the Directive consider that Member States should provide that posted temporary agency agencies should guarantee posted temporary workers the same rights as those of host country temporary workers. At the same time, we welcome the introduction of a new provision concerning subcontractors, a form of recognition of the possibility of introducing different types of chain liability. This allows Member States to require an undertaking to enter into agreements only with subcontractors guaranteeing compliance with the same pay conditions that the former undertaking must comply with. But we must also admit that we share the disappointment expressed by both the LO, the FTF and the academics in their response to the Commission's draft, because the Commission's proposal does not immediately meet the ambition of introducing a genuine principle of equal pay for equal work at the same workplace. The Commission's draft, probably for political reasons alone, is a minimum review. Therefore, it will also be crucial how the negotiations are going to take place, and it is crucial that the government is fully up to the mark to ensure that employees working in Denmark receive the same pay for the same work in the same place. We note that both the trade union movement and employers express concern that a number of the provisions comply with the principles of proportionality and subsidiarity. In particular, on the concept of pay and on the limitation of Member States' access to equal treatment for posted workers with domestic workers, there is a need to be extra vigilant. A process is already under way in the Committee on European Affairs and the Committee on Employment and Social Affairs, a process in which we in the Social Democratic Party are currently very much involved. As I said, we recognise the good intentions of the proposers, but we also find it rather strange that a motion for a resolution containing the five very specific points should be put forward for the mandate. The wording of the motion for a resolution that attaches decisive importance to all five points in the forthcoming negotiations will unnecessarily bind the government and can, in the utmost consequence, tighten the way for possible improvements to the Commission's draft, and this will lock the government to the unattainable, because, as I said, the position of the Social Democratic Party is that we are pleased with the revision of the directive. We would have liked the Commission to have gone further, but we hope that this can be achieved in the negotiations. The individual objectives of the resolution are in isolation excellent, but it is important for us that the government has room for manoeuvre in the forthcoming negotiations. Against this background, we in the Socialist Group cannot support the motion for a resolution. As regards motion for a resolution No. B 172, we again recognise in the Social Democratic Party the good intentions of the proposers in relation to safeguarding workers' rights in the EU Member States. Social democracy is in favour of the free movement of labour, but of course provided that foreign workers are employed on Danish pay and working conditions. In addition, taxes, social contributions and VAT must, of course, be paid in accordance with the rules in force either in the country of origin or in Denmark. We therefore recognise the relevance of the resolution in this context. Nevertheless, we reject the motion for a resolution on a social protocol for two reasons. Firstly, we do not think that the comments in the motion for a resolution are fair. The comments indicate that workers' rights, as the rules are today, are not guaranteed at all in the European Union, for example. It is not true that employers in the Member States are completely free to play, as stated in the comments. In addition, fundamental social rights, including conflict law, are already guaranteed in the Charter of Fundamental Rights, such as: Nor is it apparent from the comments made. Secondly, the fundamental social democratic policy is that the re-orientation of European policy should not be based on fragmented resolutions, but should be determined by a broad consensus and taking into account Denmark's overall relationship with the European Union, especially if we want to change treaties or write new protocols. In view of this, we cannot support the motion for a resolution. B 172.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426150550	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Kenneth Kristensen Berth, Danish People's Party. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426150602	Thank you. I would like to start by thanking the authors of the motion for a resolution for taking the initiative for this important debate in the Parliamentary Assembly today. The proposers have put forward two proposals for a parliamentary resolution, both of which contain important considerations in relation to the future protection of workers. As you know, the Posting of Workers Directive is before a much-needed review, but it is also clear that the revision proposed by the Commission does not meet the wishes that could have been expressed in advance. The Commission itself has suggested that a future review of the Posting of Workers Directive would address the question of how long someone may be temporarily posted, but this is neglected in the draft review. However, one can reach as far as to conclude that it will probably be more than 24 months, which is not so much temporary. In addition, the new draft directive, as far as the importance of the directive's concept of pay is concerned, shifts competences from the nation states to the European Court of Justice. In the motion for a resolution, Enhedslisten proposes to equip the government with a mandate for the negotiations on a revision of the Posting of Workers Directive, which implies a number of improvements in relation to what is existing, both as regards the existing directive as the proposed revision. Basically, the Danish People's Party believes that people working in Denmark should be subject to the same rules on pay, working time and employment conditions as apply to the other labour market in Denmark, and this is also the basis for the agreement between the Danish Employers' Association and the LO on posting in 1992. The Commission's proposal for a new directive interferes with what has hitherto been to be regarded as national matters, namely the issue of the concept of pay, and it is positive that the Enhedslist is critical of this in its proposal. It must be maintained that, of course, it cannot be attributed to the European Union in a directive that is enshrined in the Treaty and maintained as national matters. Afterwards, Enhedslisten clarifies matters which from a Danish optics seem obvious, namely among others. the right to strike and other collective action. The Danish People's Party fully shares the dissatisfaction with the interpretation of these facts by the European Court of Justice, as expressed by the verdicts in the Viking Line and Laval case. It therefore makes good sense to clarify that the conditions which should apply to the Danish labour market must of course also apply to companies covered by the Posting of Workers Directive. As you know, the Danish Government already argued in the Viking Line and Laval cases that the EU had no fundamental right to interfere in conflict law. It is therefore also logical, in the view of the Danish People's Party, that this fundamental position should be repeated with regard to the Commission in the revision of the Posting of Workers Directive. It may also be part of history that the Employment Committee currently has the proposal for a revision of the Posting of Workers Directive for a proximity check and that an opinion should be given on it on 2 May, and here it is perhaps not entirely unlikely that the committee will come to the conclusion that the Commission should be equipped with a yellow card. The first proposal from the Enhedslisten can therefore be supported by the Danish People's Party. As far as the second proposal from the Enhedslisten on the introduction of a social protocol in the Treaty of Lisbon is concerned, we understand the ideas behind it, in other words the desire to protect employees in Denmark and, in so far as other countries are concerned, from social dumping. The only question is whether it is the right medicine in relation to the diagnosed disease. Basically, I believe that the Danish People's Party and Enhedslisten agree that the ambition is less EU regulation than more EU regulation, but with the introduction of such a social protocol as Enhedslisten wants, we risk opening up more EU regulation, in other words for the EU to take on greater responsibility in the labour market area. Moreover, it is not self-evident that just because, for example, we in Denmark, as the authors of the motion for a resolution want, ask for a social protocol with the wishes we may now have for such a one, the social protocol which will be reached when it is completed will also express what the Enhedslisten or other parties may want. It may well end up being a social protocol which, de facto, does not strengthen the legal position of employees but weakens it. That depends on the content. Furthermore, it should also be borne in mind that if you first move on to the track that says that you need a protocol to deal with a specific problem, then you may also risk that a number of other stakeholders will want various other protocols to deal with specific issues that are important to them, thereby really launching an unfortunate process towards more EU regulation, and that is certainly not the starting point of the Danish People's Party. The question is whether it would not really be more appropriate to attack the issue raised by Enhedslisten through a Danish reservation. In the past, we in the Parliamentary Assembly have discussed the issue of a 'welfare reserve', and in our opinion it would make very good sense to tackle the problem of social dumping under the same heading. So I would recommend the proposers to consider that. So although we in the Danish People's Party understand the reasons for the proposers of the proposal for a social protocol, we are therefore somewhat reluctant to support this second proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426152710	Thank you to the rapporteur. And the next rapporteur is Mr. Carsten Bach, Liberal Alliance.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426152719	Thank you. In fact, the Liberal Alliance's rapporteur, Mrs Christina Egelund, is prevented, which is why I am allowed to stand here today in the Chair and make her speech as rapporteur. Motion for a resolution B 171 is divided into two parts and aims to strengthen the fight against so-called social dumping, and to reflect on the balance between European and national competences. The Liberal Alliance disagrees with the interpretation of what the resolution calls social dumping. It is important to distinguish between healthy competition and the circumvention of rules, and for us freedom of movement creates opportunities for individuals and is also a benefit to society as a whole. This also applies to the working groups which are experiencing greater competition in their particular sector. In addition, we disagree with the scale of the problem. According to the Danish Employers' Association's Labour Market Report, three out of four foreigners work under Danish agreement. The current Posting of Workers Directive also already requires uniform conditions for minimum wages, working hours, paid holidays, protection of pregnant women, etc. etc. In addition, an enforcement of the directive has been adopted to further ensure compliance with the rules, but we have not yet seen the full effects of this, as it will only have to be final and fully implemented later this year. However, like the Enhedslisten, the Liberal Alliance is also concerned with the case law of the European Court of Justice and the balance between national and European competences. Overall, however, the Liberal Alliance cannot vote in favour of motion for a resolution B 171 nor in favour of B 172.

So it is not always in the European Court of Justice that the dog is buried. After all, the European Court of Justice acts on the basis of the scope left by the legislators in the form of the European Parliament and the Member States in the form of imprecise terms when, in negotiating situations, it is not possible to agree on precise definitions. So it is important that we do not just blame the EU institutions when decisions are taken by the European Court of Justice, which we do not like, because we on the part of the Member States are often very much involved in creating these situations ourselves. It is about having the tools to ensure that when there are foreign workers, that is to say posted workers, that is to say, if they come individually and look for work here as a migrant worker, as it is said, then in principle there are no problems • we should have the opportunity to ensure a level playing field. There is therefore no point in ignoring the views of the bourgeois parties and the minister, because the Court of Justice has prevented us from doing so with a series of judgments which we have mentioned here. Therefore, it is insanely important that we establish what principles we are working on when we are going to enter into negotiations on this Posting of Workers Directive. We simply need to ensure that the Member States and their trade unions, if it is now the trade unions that manage it, have the right to demand that employees be covered by the same rules as employees in host countries' own companies. It must be stated that the Member States have the right to implement this requirement of equal treatment with the means that each host State now wishes to use, that is. through legislation, which many EU countries use, or by collective agreements or a combination of both. We have then chosen here in Denmark that it is the social partners and that it is collective agreements. We must ensure that the trade unions in the individual Member States are given the freedom to start collective action, as it says in a fine word, that is, conflict law, in order to ensure that this principle of equal pay for equal work is applied in accordance with the rules currently in force in that Member State. We must make it clear that the Court of Justice does not have jurisdiction to interpret the legality of collective action initiated for the purpose I have just described. And then we have to make it clear that when it comes to defining pay regulations and other provisions relating to employment conditions, it is only national competence. It should have been a matter of course for members of the Danish Parliament to say without further ado: yes, of course we must have it. For that was the prerequisite for Danish membership in 1972. We are so old that we can remember the slogan: The nuts we break ourselves. It was a joint statement by the then chairman of the Danish Employers' Association and the then chairman of the LO who said: "Don't worry, because membership of the EEC, as it was then called, will not affect conflict law and our collective agreement model. It was a clear promise that was made and it was a very important prerequisite for the success of the Danes in voting in favour of membership of the European Community. But history has just shown that that promise did not hold water, which some people also warned against then. It should then be a matter of course, and, indeed, what would be more natural for a Danish Parliament, when one now finds that the promise did not keep water, that the principles have not been respected, than to make those demands to the other countries of the European Union and to the Commission; especially when it is a principle that Danish governments have maintained throughout as recently as in the Viking Line and Laval judgments in 2007, when Denmark made a contribution to the matter, which clearly stated that it is not the EU's competence to interfere in conflict law. It was then told by the Court that Denmark was therefore terribly wrong there. So even then, we should have put our heels in and protested against signing the Treaty of Lisbon. You did not, but now you have a chance, because this Posting of Workers Directive is under negotiation. But then there is a majority in this Parliament who will not. It's the government, it's the Left, the Liberal Alliance, the Radical Left, the Conservatives. The Socialists do not want to go back to that in a few moments. What is surprising in the argument is that it does not really mean that there is a problem, and if there is a problem, it can be resolved within the EU rules in force through negotiations on the basis of the Commission's proposals. We simply do not want to deal with the real content of the judicial decisions we are discussing here. Let us just take them very briefly: the Viking Line judgment was handed down in December 2007. What was it about? It was about the Court of Justice agreeing with itself that the Court of Justice had the right to interfere in a Finnish ongoing conflict. That was quite a shock report. I will not tire the House of all the legal subtleties that the Court of Justice used. Unfortunately, it was probably right somewhere when you look at the EU rules, as they are screwed up. There, however, the Court of Justice ruled that it is entitled to intervene in the European Court of Justice. A week later, the Court of Justice entered into the conflict initiated by Swedish construction workers in order to ensure that Latvian construction workers in a posted Latvian company would be covered by the same agreement as Swedish construction workers were covered by, in other words, completely in line with the basic position in Denmark on the incompatibility of conflict law with EU rules. The Court of Justice said: No, it is not acceptable because this Posting of Workers Directive only allows minimum wage to be charged, and because, incidentally, there was no legislation on minimum wage in Sweden, yes, for that reason too, the Swedish conflict was illegal. However, what was interesting about the judgment was that it stated that the requirements referred to in the Posting of Workers Directive were the minimum wage, overtime payments and a few other more socially-conditioned claims. That is the maximum that must be demanded. Nevertheless, we can hear the Minister and his colleagues stand up and say that we really do not have any problems that we cannot solve. It is just very interesting why a change of the posting law was made in Denmark if there was no problem with conflict law. It was probably because there was a problem when it came to it. That is where the Laval judgment sent a shock wave throughout the European trade union movement because it turned completely upside down how this Posting of Workers Directive had so far been perceived, i.e. that the scraped minimum requirements set out in that directive are a maximum. It is not legal to conflict, nor to legislate on anything in relation to posted workers who go beyond it. This also means that Member States and their trade unions have been prevented from ensuring a level playing field. That's how simple the problem is. The next judgment was the Rüffert judgment, and it was against a German Land, Lower Saxony, which was told not to require a Polish service provider to comply with the local agreements in this area for German construction workers if the Polish builder concerned was to have a contract with the Land. That was a very clear decision. In other words, the German Land was not allowed to ensure a level playing field by means of the legislation and agreements that were now in place. Then came the Luxembourg judgment. There it was a Member State that was convicted. Luxembourg was told by the Court of Justice that it was prohibited if the agreements in force in Luxembourg were to apply to all companies, not only those from Luxembourg, but also those from other EU countries. So once again a clear decision that we are not allowed to ensure a level playing field. So how can you avoid that relationship? I simply do not understand that. We can only do that if we are actually prepared to see that the minimum wage is good enough, and then we can do it. But there are many other important things in an agreement other than the minimum wage, and as Mr. Karsten Hønge also mentioned, the Danish minimum wage agreements are certainly not geared at all to ensuring a level playing field on that level. So unless you think it is okay with such little social dumping at minimum wage level, it is incomprehensible that we do not support the proposal we have put forward. Then the Minister is sorry that we bring it down to the Chamber. I think that is an inappropriate remark. If we in Parliament are not allowed to discuss what mandate and what objectives a government should pursue in negotiations on such an important issue, then I do not know where else we should be allowed to discuss it. There I agree with the Danish People's Party and thank you for your support on this point. What other options would the Enhedslisten have in order to promote the views that we believe should be promoted? Because that is not the way the negotiations are conducted in the Committee on European Affairs. It is just that if you do not find a majority against the government's proposal for a mandate, it will slip through. So I think that was a very inappropriate remark which does not give a very good indication of a more democratic and open approach to the discussions on the European Union. Then there is the position of the Socialists on both proposals. I have to say that this is an extremely ambiguous affair, because it is in this way in agreement with the individual points. In any case, we have not heard that there should be just one of those people with whom we did not agree. So what's the problem? Then the next battle had to decide how far we could get with it. But then we get such a process excuse that we should not bind the government. Well, if you mean anything by any suggestions, you will of course try to tie the government up on it. That is also the first condition for a government to support the seven other Member States, which have clearly told the Commission that we must now have a review of the Posting of Workers Directive, which guarantees equal pay for equal work. The Commission's proposal by no means does that. There we are, of course, very much in agreement, I think, the Social Democrats and the Enhedslisten. I hope that agreement will also bear fruit tomorrow when we discuss the Commission's proposal and what we do with it. But I think we should be supported if we do not agree with the content of this. That the proposal for a social protocol cannot be supported, I have been astonished at the short comments that have already been made. I just don't get it. The Danish Social Democratic Party, the Danish LO, has met with its Nordic sister and brother parties and organisations and supported the demands of the European trade union movement that we should have such a social protocol. But when it comes to a specific position in this Chamber, you will not be in it. Frankly, I think that's too lax. On the social protocol and then we are over in the slightly more technical discussion, but I would like to say it anyway: this is not about what Mr. Rasmus Jarlov has read into it or tried to read into it. He then came to a certain realization of this as the questions progressed. We are not trying to describe a large number of specific requirements. The social protocol is simply about stating that if there is a conflict between professional and social rights, for example. the right of conflict or the right of Member States to protect themselves against social dumping, and freedom of movement, i.e. economic freedoms, must take precedence over professional and social rights. Then there's no point in talking about balance. After all, there is no balance when the Court of Justice has made it clear that we must not impose a level playing field when it comes to posted undertakings. The main purpose of this social protocol is to interpret the specific provisions of the Treaty which have been applied by the Court of Justice when it handed down the judgments in which it intervened in conflict law and intervened in the right of Member States to protect themselves against social dumping. That is why it is so a greeting to the Danish People's Party that such a social protocol will not open up more EU regulation. It will open up less regulation because it will prevent the Court from interfering in areas where it is interfering today. Finally, I would just like to thank you for a good debate. As I said, it was nice to clarify that the bourgeois wing has made the choice. Yes, growth will suffer if we now place so much emphasis on the social rights that Enhedslisten and other parties would like. That's a clear message. It's easy to feel. So thank you for the clear message and thank you for the parties that support the proposal. This is the alternative and the Socialist People's Party, and it is partly the Danish People's Party that supports the proposal for the Posting of Workers Directive. Thank you to these parties for that support, and thank you for the debate.

After all, the model is based on flexibility and is a safety net flexibility, because the safety of fixed pay and working conditions for employees goes hand in hand with flexibility for employers when it comes to the possibility of hiring and firing employees. These are conditions which are much better for both employees and employers compared to the rest of Europe, largely, at least if you look down south of the border. That is why it undermines the model when employers employ workers for wages well below the agreed minimum wage; it undermines the model when foreign companies do not pay the VAT, tax, etc. to which they are obliged; and it undermines the model when the state does not fulfil its role, which is to be a safety net under those who become unemployed, and also provide training while people are unemployed, and not least to check that there is no cheating and cheating on the things businesses and employers have to live up to. The Liberal Alliance is opposed to chain liability as a general principle. In addition to requiring a private undertaking to carry out a regulatory task, it will also create an unfortunate incentive for subcontractors. Nor do we support chain liability on the part relating to pay and working conditions, and we believe that we have a fairly well-functioning labour law system that must interpret and enforce the agreements that are now concluded. To place all the responsibility on the contractor is to undermine the role of the trade union movement in ensuring that companies comply with the agreements they enter into with their employees. The obligations placed on the contractor by this proposal are, for us, unfair and we will therefore not vote in favour of the proposal.

To this I would say that we have now had several years and several contractual negotiation situations to implement something that works and has not succeeded. Social dumping is flourishing, and it is despite some of the few initiatives that have been taken. The problems have not diminished. We have just seen the report now from the research centre at the University of Copenhagen, FAOS, which clearly demonstrates that the problem has not been solved, but that, on the contrary, it still has a huge scope and still poses a major threat to the Danish labour market and to Danish employees. Social dumping, which cheats on wages, but also on tax and VAT settlements, remains the greatest threat to our regulated labour market, which is based on agreements on wages and working conditions between trade unions and employers. And since our well-tested labour market model is also a cornerstone of the whole welfare society, social dumping, of course, by its very nature is also a threat to the welfare society. So let me stress once again that with the motion for a resolution tabled by Enhedslisten, there is no indication whatsoever that we are legislating on contractual relations. We are just trying to say that there has to be a place to go with the bill when we find that there are some who either cheat the employees or cheat the state for VAT and tax etc. It is therefore also important to find the most effective means of combating social dumping. And we actually believe that chain liability is one of the most effective means. This is to ensure that the companies that offer a task think very well before it is a very good incentive for a company to look after a possible subcontractor by naturally asking for calculations that show that there is actually work for contractual wages and that you have order in the accounts, so that VAT and tax are also settled and what else needs to be settled. And with the knowledge of a main contractor or contractor to its territory, such a firm will soon know whether there are owls in the bog when tenders are coming from subcontractors or subcontractors, as the enforcement directive says, and which, of course, cover completely broadly. Do you not think the main contractors and main suppliers will secure against losses further down the chain through the requirement of a guarantee, i.e. a bank guarantee? It will only be one way to make sure that it is understood in the sense that if a subcontractor says that it will not be asked, yes, he will of course not get a chance. And I wonder whether a main contractor and contractor would prefer to use companies that are in order, perhaps in reality, through membership of an employers' association? There are some who would argue that chain liability by law is contrary to the Danish labour market model I have mentioned that but in our best opinion it is nonsense. There is not a word in the B proposal to legislate on the content of the agreements. What chain liability means is that the companies which use subcontractors and subcontractors respectively have a responsibility for ensuring that the agreements or agreements between the company and the employees which they will hopefully have signed up to, work under and have been negotiated through their organizations are not undermined and that the very same main suppliers and main contractors respectively are not tempted to use other firms which do not have purely flour in the bag to carry out the tasks involved in the task offered, in an attempt to make their own offer cheaper and thus to impose unfair competition on other companies, other firms. That is why, in fact, we also think that this B proposal is much better than the draft law put forward by the government on a labour market fund for posted workers, with the reservation that it has only just been put forward today, and that so far we have only seen the draft bill itself, where all the employers who comply with their obligations in terms of pay, etc., are apparently required to pay for those employers who do not comply with their obligations. I find that incomprehensible. I find it quite incomprehensible that the Minister, with a past in the Danish Employers' Association, may think that it is fair to, among others. many of the DA's own member companies in the organizations which are now affiliated to the DA. In fact, we think that the introduction of chain liability is much fairer and, as I said, a far, far more effective means of combating social dumping in the Danish labour market.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426172022	The debate is open and the first speaker is the Minister for the Environment and Food.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426172042	Thank you for that, President. Thank you to the Liberal Alliance for tabling the motion for a resolution. Nearly a year ago, the Folketing discussed a motion for a resolution from the then opposition to amend the river basin law. Today, we are again discussing almost the same proposal, but a lot has happened since we took over government power. One of the things that has not changed, however, is the big focus on watercourses: on large streams, small streams, streams with too much water and streams with too little water. The river basin is an area in which there are many opinions and interests to take into account. That is why it was one of the first areas we started to look at when the present government came into being, because we will not stand by and watch good agricultural land be taken out of production, because watercourses cannot be sufficiently directed away from land. Watercourses must be maintained so that they can ensure a proper drainage of water, but this must of course be done so that at the same time nature and the environment are taken into account. We're doing a lot of work on this. Firstly, a letter has been sent to the municipalities stating that they must ensure that public watercourses are maintained so that they comply with the requirements laid down in the regulations. However, public watercourses are only approx. 25% of the open Danish streams. Compared with the remaining approx. 75% It is the private broad-owners who have the obligation to maintain the watercourses. Secondly, a research group has been set up to make recommendations on how crop cutting should be carried out in order to take into account, as far as possible, both drainage and natural and environmental water quality requirements. The recommendations must form the basis for the Danish Nature Agency to draw up a proper guide on crop cutting. The research team's draft is on the way in consultation with stakeholders. The guidance itself is expected to be ready sometime after the summer break. This does not mean that new methods of crop cutting will solve all problems, but it is a step in the right direction. Thirdly, among the bourgeois parties, an agreement has been reached on a food and agricultural package stating that the criteria for when a watercourse should be included in water planning must be reviewed so that narrow, flat and dug watercourses should not be part of the river basin plans in the future. The municipalities and local water councils will be involved in that work. Once the results of these initiatives are available and come from the municipalities' evaluation of their climate adjustment plans, we have a good overview of where there may still be problems with the discharge of water. I will take that knowledge with me, considering at a later stage the launching of a major investigation work in which I will review the existing legislation that has an impact on water dissipation, so that we can better safeguard both country and city against flooding and dumping, so that people do not lose great values, whether on land or in city. We have long focused on the need for climate protection for our cities, but it is important that we also address the need for climate adjustment in the open country. The Liberal Alliance's proposal contains three sub-proposals. Watercourses shall primarily serve drainage purposes. Such a change in the law would mean that in all watercourses the watering interest would always have to be given priority, no matter how great or small this interest is. This will mean that Denmark will have difficulty in meeting our obligations in relation to the EU Water Framework, Habitats and Birds Protection Directives, unless we implement significant compensatory measures. In the worst case scenario, the cost of compensatory measures can amount to a very considerable amount if we are to prevent deterioration of the aquatic environment. It is also important to bear in mind that even if we resolve a drainage problem locally in one place, we may risk moving the problems to another place further down the river. Furthermore, the motion for a resolution states that the river basin law must be amended so that the municipality must prove that it is necessary to derogate from the requirements of the regulations for maintenance for environmental reasons. It should be stressed that it is already the case that according to current administrative practice, the municipality must provide the professional basis for determining whether a change in watercourse maintenance would harm or not harm the habitat types and species designated by a Natura 2000 site to protect. In the administration of the Watercourse Act, it is also the responsibility of the municipality to assess whether a modified watercourse maintenance will destroy or damage breeding or resting areas for a number of species covered by the Habitats Directive. The final element of the proposal is to remove watercourses from section 3 of the Nature Conservation Act and move them to the Watercourse Act, so that the protection of watercourses is based on a basis that allows the adaptation of watercourses' watering capacity based on an overall balance of considerations of drainage, nature, environment and climate adaptation. The reason why watercourses are part of section 3 of the Nature Conservation Act is the protection of watercourses as habitats for wild plant and animal life in interaction with the surrounding bogs, meadows and lakes, as well as ensuring the open course of the watercourses as green corridors through the landscape, so that the streams are not completed, dug up, refurbished or similar. The protection helps to meet the requirements of the Habitats Directive to protect selected habitats and habitats for a number of species. The protection provided for in Article 3 of the Nature Conservation Act does not mean that changes in conditions in the protected watercourses cannot be made without derogation. General maintenance of watercourses, including maintenance in accordance with the river basin regulations, and maintenance of drains do not require derogation, but regulation of watercourses such as: changing the profile or deepening does. In such cases, the municipality has the power to take decisions. There is therefore a good correlation between the rules of the Watercourse Act and the Nature Conservation Act. A year ago, the Left was one of the authors of the motion. Today we cannot support the proposal. This is because we're changing the law on watercourses now will be like reading the end of the book first. I am not saying that the government will never change the law on watercourses. I say that the time is certainly not right right now. However, as I said, I am considering initiating legislative preparatory work on a possible amendment of the river basin law once we have seen the results of the initiatives already initiated by the government.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426173912	The motion for a resolution we are debating today reiterates three of the elements of B 145 from last year and, in short, is that watercourses should serve mainly drainage purposes rather than nature and environmental purposes. Denmark, for example, has. no longer an environment minister with one resort. Firstly, the proposal proposes a unilateral clarification of the river basin law so that it is clear that watercourses are intended to serve drainage purposes. In other words, dewatering interests must take precedence over environmental considerations. The balance must be tipped, one must understand. We basically disagree with that. Watercourses must be provided both for farmers, for nature, for anglers and for the environment. The 1983 Watercourse Act has just been put in the world to weigh those considerations, and we are not quite interested in changing that balance. We want a law on watercourses to resolve the conflicts that may arise between the various considerations. It is important to make it clear that drainage considerations and considerations of the environment and nature are not always contradictory. Often it is possible to strike a reasonable balance, and that is what our law on watercourses must contribute to. By the way, several of our municipalities have shown that they can do so. It is also important, of course, that in this context we consider how to spread the best practice to the other municipalities in the country. In this way, we can ensure the balance between the environment and drainage throughout the country. I welcome the fact that increased rainfall is a challenge. Firstly, the changes are a hint that we should not relax the ambitious climate change course, in which the government has unfortunately proved to be champions. Finally, it has to be said that it will be difficult to comply with our EU commitments if we allow concern for our environment to give way to watering down. It can have major consequences for Denmark and can end up in a bill for agriculture and for the environment if, for example, the European Union does not want to approve our rural programme because we are relaxing the protection of the aquatic environment. No one is interested in that. The Liberal Alliance also wants the burden of proof to lie with the municipality in the specific cases where there are doubts as to whether watering considerations should take precedence over environmental considerations. In this context, I should just like to say that it has always been the responsibility of the municipalities to provide the necessary basis for establishing whether a drainage of a river can be kept within the framework of EU law. Finally, the Liberal Alliance wishes to remove section 3 of the protection of watercourses. The Socialists do not believe that § 3 protection is a barrier to the maintenance of watercourses. In the event of major changes, derogations can be sought, as the Minister also explained in his speech, to ensure drainage. At the same time, it will have consequences for nature protection if protection is removed, and it will go out and animal and plant life. We want to help look at the river basin law, but it just needs to be in the right balance, where the environment and drainage considerations are better balanced with each other. ParlaMint-DK_20160426180659	Thank you for that, President. Realities speak their clear language. In many parts of the country there are major problems along the streams of floods and insidious flooding of the terrain. In recent years, there have been higher amounts of water, including so-called monster showers, while the watering capacity of many watercourses has deteriorated. It has to end badly in the form of flooding and dumping. It costs society and the pilot owners affected enormous losses, and for us in the Left it is vital that we increase our focus on the need for water to be directed more effectively to the sea. Fortunately, this consideration can easily go hand in hand with regard to nature, the environment and good biodiversity. There is no need to contrast it. That is why I very much welcome the motion for a resolution tabled by the Liberal Alliance as an opportunity for a necessary debate on this important issue. In recent years, the focus of river management has shifted somewhat from the need for water drainage to the need to focus more on the environmental aspects of watercourses. This has meant raising the water mirror in many streams. This has resulted in a reduction in porridge cutting, less clean-up and many other measures leading to a reduction in water flow and thus an increase in water level. So this whole terrain thing, it's just a myth. There has actually been an increase in the water mirror in many places. We have measurements on that. In addition to these drainage conditions, it is a fact that the annual rainfall has increased by approx. 100 mm over the last 100 years, and leading hydrologists predict that the amount of water will increase further in the coming years. In the Left, we believe that this must be the past of the very devastating floods of cottage areas, residential areas and agricultural land due to inadequate maintenance of watercourses. Effective drainage of the land is a fundamental condition for the possibility of farming in this country at all, and I know a lot of people who would like to spend the money that agriculture brings home to Denmark and who are happy with the many jobs generated by agriculture. High water levels also affect the drainage of soils, not only along the creeks themselves, but also well away from the watercourses themselves. But it is not only agriculture that needs watering down the watercourses. It is also important for forestry to prevent tree roots from being destroyed or limited in their growth of increased water levels. In many areas along the watercourses, beautiful solitary trees drown, and many gardens and cellars suffer from floods. Figuratively speaking, the terrain along many streams is becoming like a sponge that is constantly soaked. And anyone who knows a sponge that is soaked knows that there isn't much reserve to suck any more water when rain comes. That is why we have the problem. Last week I had the pleasure of having a visit to Central Jutland. Our Minister for the Environment and Food was on a field visit where we among other things. highlighted the importance of directing water to the sea to avoid flooding. The minister met at the banks of Gudenåen, among others. Bjerringbro and Omregns Sportsfisherforening, which presented its very serious proposal for intelligent crop cutting and for improving watercourse maintenance. The Minister signed for the proposal on the spot and listened intensely to the need to lead the water better away and there is a great need. I have met similar constructive approaches in other sports associations in the middle Jutland. Sports fishermen are tired of not being able to get roughly dry-shoeed down to the river's shore to fish. The government is well on its way to meeting the needs. The former Minister for the Environment, Mrs Eva Kjer Hansen, has sent a relevant shepherd letter to the municipalities to point out the need to implement porridge cutting as the water rises. In addition, the government has launched a study on optimised porridge cutting. But we in the Left are fully aware that this is not enough. The water must be better to the sea, and in many places in the watercourses plugged into the hole, if I may say so, in the form of deposits that have been placed by veins. The watering capacity suffers, so it also requires action. I am pleased that the Minister has indicated today that, having seen the outcome of what has been initiated and when we have had an evaluation of what has been undertaken on crop cutting, he is ready to start a legislative preparatory work, and that is what is needed. We need to continue working on the area. We don't have the full overview yet. I would call for us to try to land this in committee with a report calling on the government to start work on modernising the river basin law when the evaluation is available. In this context, there is a need for a good dialogue with the municipalities, with the landowners, with agriculture, forestry and sports fishermen about other nature-interested people. In the future, municipalities will need a wide range of instruments that can be used when the water rises above the designated cow. The best thing is that the objective of more efficient drainage can easily be achieved without compromising on nature. This requires only a good dialogue and intelligent and modern means of action. I look forward to the fact that in the coming months we are working on a cross-party basis to keep our focus on this extremely important issue.
