ParlaMint-DK_20160426182442	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no more brief comments right now. The next speaker is the rapporteur for the Enhedslisten, Mrs Maria Reumert Gjerding. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426182455	What is a stream? That is really the key issue when we deal with this motion for a resolution by the Liberal Alliance. For the Liberal Alliance there is no doubt: the purpose of the watercourses is to serve a drainage purpose. In other words, the purpose of the watercourses is to water down the farmer's fields, and the Liberal Alliance wants this to be clarified in the law on watercourses, just as the law needs to be amended, so that watercourses are more zealously cleaned up, the watercourse floor is excavated and water plants are removed. There are also those who believe that watercourses are something other than drainage channels for agriculture. Some people believe that watercourses are nature. There are people who like to go for a walk and experience streams that are full of life. There are people who like to fish a trout or a salmon in the Danish streams. There are also people who for decades have made a huge effort to recreate life in the Danish streams. There are volunteers who have laid out spawning grounds to restore spawning sites in many places that have been destroyed by extensive watercourse purification and excavation of watercourse floors. There are people who have exposed fish fry to get the fish that have had cramped conditions for years, because streams have been refurbished, refurbished and excavated, back into the streams. There are those who believe that it is important that we as a society work to improve the aquatic environment in our watercourses, because nature is important to all of us, whether you are an angler, a tourist or just an ordinary Dane. There are also those who point out that a living watercourse with aquatic plants is much better at converting nitrogen, which means that by doing about the opposite of what the Liberal Alliance is proposing here, we will have better opportunities to meet the important environmental objectives, which include: I would like to ask the Commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the Commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work has not been accepted by the Commission. The Liberal Alliance is using a main argument in their resolution, which I think I need to address in my rapporteur's speech, and it is about watercourse purification being important to avoid flooding residential areas and holiday homes. Water areas, i.e. meadows and valleys are, and have always been, those places in nature that have managed to withhold water from the surroundings, because wetlands, as I have said before, act as a sponge. When it rains a lot, the wetlands absorb the water, the meadow or the river valley, and then the water is slowly released to the surroundings. What has happened in recent decades? There many of these valleys have been included, the meadows have been converted into farmland, they have been drained and cultivated, so the stream has become a narrow stream that should lead the water away. We have therefore removed large areas of natural water buffers, which is the best word I can find on the landscape, and it is therefore large wetlands which provide the service free of charge to prevent flooding of other agricultural land and cities. And now the problems arise because of the removal of nature's own ability to avoid floods, and when climate change increases the amount of rainfall and many years of farming have caused the land to sit down, as it is called the land surface has sunk so much that the soil's humus content has burned off due to the agricultural drive, and heavy machines have packed the land and drains are clogged, then you get floods. If you solve the problem of watercourse maintenance, you do not remove the water; on the contrary, you send it more quickly downstream. And there are often, especially around our coasts, cottage areas and urban areas, which will then be flooded. So what you want to deal with with with this resolution is not being dealt with; on the contrary, you run the risk of aggravating the problem, and you do so even in a way that costs the municipalities immensely much money. This has also been acknowledged in other countries. For example: In England and Central Europe, in connection with the large floods that have occurred, it has been recognised that the lack of delays in the upstream water systems is a factor in the floods. That is why the only sensible solution in the situation we face, which we all regret, namely that we have climate change, which increases rainfall levels, is simply to restore nature's own lag basins,ådales, meadows to ensure a climate adjustment in which both nature and respect for all sorts of other agricultural land can go hand in hand. Thank you for the floor.

None of the reasons may bear the full blame, but all probably still bear some of the blame. It is therefore also important that the legislative preparatory work be undertaken as soon as possible. We cannot here at Christiansborg solve all the challenges of the country by simply talking about it far into the evening and sometimes even far out into the night. The product here must not be the debate alone. Changes also need to be made. I am, of course, pleased, and I have also said this in the past, that a shepherd's letter has been sent from the Ministry to the municipalities, and I am pleased with the audit work on the crop cutting guide. But I do not think that is enough and there are great values at stake. KL and others point out that a change of law is necessary to move forward from where we are now. I think that we in the Folketing should comply with this and I think that we should start the necessary process as soon as possible. ParlaMint-DK_20160426191425	Thank you for that, President. I would also like to thank the alternative for tabling the motion for a resolution. Overall, the government agrees with the aim of the proposal, but we do not agree with the tools proposed by the alternative. The government is happy to help with guidance for public purchasers who want to buy in sustainably, but it must be up to the individual municipalities, regions and government authorities to decide how they will specifically organise their purchases. At the same time, we note that the fisheries, aquaculture and retail sector are already engaged in a wide range of initiatives that promote the demand and production of sustainable fish and shellfish. The Ministry of the Environment and Food is also preparing a guide that will make it easier for public authorities to buy local and sustainable seafood. Following the timetable, the guidance will be ready as early as July this year. The government will do its part to disseminate information on counselling; it can, among other things, do so. happen via the website, the responsible buyer in Partnership for Public Green Purchases and in the newsletter from the Sustainable Purchasing Forum. When the government does not see a need to make demands, this is also due to the fact that the market itself is well on the way. The vast majority of fishing for human consumption and industrial fishing in Denmark are therefore already either sustainability certified or on the way to becoming so. For example: was more than 60%. of the fish for human consumption landed in Denmark in 2015, MSC certified. The association Denmark's Fisheries Association expects that up to 80%. of the Danish fishing will be MSC-certified within a few years. And Danish Aquaculture, which is the association of Danish fish farmers, has among other things. The aim is that the entire Danish conventional production of freshwater trout should be certified by ASC. The retail trade is also well in the process of ensuring that all fish and shellfish sold to consumers are sustainable. Danish Supermarket has set the goal that the full range of products with seafood must be sustainable. And earlier this month Lidl announced that all their wild caught fish should be MSC-certified in 2016, while all their farmed fish should be ASC-certified or organic in 2018. Thus, even without public procurement requirements for sustainable fish and shellfish, Danish fishing is in full swing with a restructuring that helps to avoid over-fishing and extinction of fish species. I have no doubt that the initiatives already launched in the fisheries, aquaculture and retail sectors, as well as the forthcoming guidance to public purchasers, will provide a greater supply of sustainable fish and shellfish to consumers and a more sustainable Danish fishing industry. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426191725	The motion for a resolution has been tabled here by the Alternative and aims to promote the sustainability of public kitchens by ensuring that the fish and shellfish purchased by the public sector have been fished sustainably. These could, for example, be the so-called MSC certified fish. Specifically, the alternative will require sustainable and nature-friendly fishing in public and government procurement. At the same time, the proposal states that sustainable caught fish must be required in order for canteens to earn the Food Authority's Ø label, and three different brands can be obtained depending on how much ecology is served in the canteen. The Socialists are generally in favour of promoting sustainability in public kitchens. We also see it in the private retail trade, which increases supply and increases the requirements for fishing in relation to sustainability labels. We made a great effort to spread ecology in public kitchens, which we see as a major step in the right direction in terms of spreading ecology to the whole country, so that the ecology will be for the many. At the same time, the Socialists take the state of fish stocks seriously and we want marine resources to be managed sustainably. Against this background, we would like to work to ensure that public procurement reflects that priority and ensures that fish are purchased sustainably. It is the impression of the Social Democrats that it will benefit Danish fishermen, as two thirds of the Danish fishing for human consumption is MSC-certified. This will stimulate the demand for Danish fish rather than the public procurement of pangasius or tiger shrimps from Asia, which cannot be categorized as sustainable. From this point of view, this is an interesting proposal in which we can both promote the sustainability of public kitchens, while at the same time creating jobs in fisheries in those parts of Denmark that have traditionally had many jobs in particular fisheries. Having said that, the Socialists also have a number of concerns about the proposal, which must be clarified before we can possibly support it. Firstly, it is important for us to see how this will affect the economy and expenditure of the municipalities and the state. The municipalities are very tense, and MSC certified fish are more expensive. Here it is important that we find a good balance that does not push the municipalities further, and therefore there is also a need in the committee work to get more knowledge of how it will affect the economy of the public sector whether funding needs to be found in order to implement the proposal. Secondly, we believe that it should be made quite clear what can be categorized as sustainable caught fish. For example, should the state only buy organically farmed fish, or should the state not buy farmed fish at all? Are MSC and ASC sufficiently good marks, or are there also built-in requirements for new marks, possibly with catch gear requirements? Thirdly, we believe that the comments on the state of fish stocks are a little too indefensible. The comments show that 70 per cent. of the EU's fish stocks are over-exploited and that the figure is 90%. Worldwide. There is a need to draw a more nuanced picture, as many stocks, at least according to the European Commission, are improving and more and more stocks are being fished according to the principle of maximum sustainable yield, i.e. the MSY principle. Fourthly, we also believe that the profession must be consulted in order to make the most appropriate and long-lasting arrangements. But there is also a need to clarify how the proposal will affect the industry. Against this background, we in the Social Democrats are looking forward to further work on the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426200102	Thank you to the alternative for putting forward this proposal so that we can have the discussion. There are good tactics in it, and then there are some things that the SF hopes we can discuss in committee. But I think this is a really, really important debate about sustainable fishing and for many reasons, so I would like to spend some of my speaking time on the background. After all, in 2007, the VK government privatised a common good: the fish that are in our seas. After that, the right to fish a commodity has become a speculative object, in which rights have been rented out or sold to the highest bidder, who is already the richest fishermen with large banks in their backs who are prepared to outbid the smaller fishermen at any price. Today there are too few buyers for too expensive quotas, and it costs local communities where larger and larger vessels are served by ever fewer hands. 100 ports are closed or halved and it is almost impossible for young fishermen to enter the market. To serve the same as a primary school teacher, you have to buy quotas for 30-40 million. kr. And there has been a halving of the number of jobs since 2000. It is not open to discussion whether this development is due to the privatisation of quotas, it is a fact. We have seen quota concentrations where billions have been collected in a few hands, and at the same time it has resulted in fewer jobs, closed ports and communities withering. So if you want to do something both for the sustainability of our fish in our seas, but certainly also for sustainability in local communities and for local jobs, it is sustainable fishing that is the way forward. This is where there are most local jobs. That is where the fish are landed, instead of all sorts of strange places. This is the way for some of the most threatened communities, which have been completely dependent on local fishing and are about to die out now because the port is closed and which have not yet been fully wiped out, to have a chance to survive. That is why it is so important in addition to all the good in terms of marine sustainability, fish and so on, and it will mean a huge difference for the communities that are completely dependent on the fishing and not on giant bottom trawlers, like no, not fishing sustainably and where there are incredibly many quotas that are placed in very few hands, and also with very few jobs to do. We have had a fishery where it is completely impossible for young people to get in unless they organize themselves as in the new cooperative communities, which we fortunately see as a few bright spots around. The very specific proposal contains some problems from the point of view of the SF. We like the idea. I'd like to make that clear. But there are two things. One is in relation to the MSC brand and the ASC mark: we do not think that they guarantee to a large extent gentle fishing or organically farmed fishing. I'll get back to that. The second point is that we believe that organic fishing, unlike farming, creates confusion about the concept of ecology. Compared with the first, we in the SF, together with other good forces in the Parliamentary Assembly, have long proposed a labelling scheme for nature-friendly fishing, because the MSC label only guarantees that the fish are not caught illegally or with total destruction as a result. For example: is clam scraping and bottom trawling, after all, the MSC mark. There has also been a general criticism of this. The ASC label has been criticised for promoting fish farming at the expense of local fishing. As far as my second point is concerned, organic food is characterised by the fact that production is under human control. It is also in fish farming, but not in fishing on the high seas. But SF supports what we see as the main purpose of the alternative, namely the extension of nature-friendly coastal fishing in Denmark and other countries. We believe that this is best done by means of a credible labelling system that is targeted at locally sustainable fishing, and preferably assisted by public procurement. In conclusion, I have perhaps not said this in quite as strong terms as the radical rapporteur, that I am divided between what we want to do to promote sustainable development, including through public procurement of 290 billion. kr. Every year, and the little municipal autonomy that's left in this country. I also have a soft point for that. So perhaps we could see how we are meeting some of these concerns by saying: we are redefining some quotas for sustainable fishing. We support it financially, as we already do today. We are giving much more favourable conditions so that it will be the most desirable product to buy out in today's institutions. I think we want the same thing, so I'm sure we can reach each other.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426201841	The debate is open. I give the floor to the Minister for the Environment and Food. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426201900	Thank you for that, President, and thank you to the SF for tabling the motion for a resolution. Let me begin by stating that I do not agree with the authors of the motion for a resolution that in the field of pesticides there is a need to compensate for the abolition of the peripheral zones and I do not agree with the authors of the motion for a resolution banning the spraying of section 3 areas. Firstly, the watercourses are still protected by the distance requirements imposed in connection with the authorisation of pesticides. Secondly, most of the covered section 3 areas are usually not sprayed in practice. Let me also stress that we have an excellent spray strategy, which has been agreed by a broad majority in the Danish Parliament, which acts as a majority, which also includes the SF. Nevertheless, I find it interesting to discuss the proposed elements in the light of the fact that the current pesticide strategy expires at the end of 2016. The Danish EPA is engaged in a broad stakeholder involvement in the light of the fact that the current pesticide strategy expires at the end of 2016. Let me now comment on the individual parts of the motion for a resolution. I agree that we must continue to have a restrictive authorisation system in Denmark. The cut-off criteria for the particularly problematic pesticide active substances, e.g. substances likely to be carcinogenic do not apply to co-formulants. In accordance with the Pesticides Regulation, a list of problematic excipients is to be drawn up. It is my view that co-formulants must not have properties that meet the cut-off criteria for the active substances, including carcinogenic or harmful to the foetus, reproductive or genetic systems, and endocrine disrupting properties. In addition, other excipients may be considered problematic and should therefore be excluded from use in syringes. The Danish EPA participates in the EU Working Group in order to establish criteria and process for establishing the list of problematic co-formulants. There are few pesticides approved in Denmark that contain particularly problematic active substances. These are subject to a high tax, which in fact encourages their use only if they are absolutely necessary. The charge is calculated on the classification of the finished pesticides. This means that in Denmark there is a strong incentive to choose co-formulants without health risks in order to reduce the tax. The proposers also propose the introduction of requirements for best available techniques, or what we call BAT, best usable technology, for spraying technology with a timetable for banning ineffective spraying techniques. There is already a great deal of focus today on minimising accidental releases of pesticides from pesticide syringes both in Denmark and in the rest of the EU. The Framework Directive on the sustainable use of pesticides provides for the introduction in all Member States of requirements for all pesticide spray equipment to undergo a mandatory inspection if it is to be used after November 2016. This minimises the risk of adverse effects of equipment on the environment and health. In Denmark, the first syringes were seen at the beginning of 2014 and the last must be seen at the latest by season 2017. The introduction of vision requirements means that a number of older syringes will be decommissioned. It is expected that a number of farmers will buy new syringes, join machine communities or have the spray work done by machinery stations that use new and intelligent syringes to a large extent. I therefore expect the existing farm spray park to be modernised. I would also like to remind you that since 2010 farmers have been able to apply for subsidies for investment in new technology with the aim of reducing pesticide consumption. Since 2010, approx. 180 million kr. in support of this. The government wants to make it attractive for farmers to invest in new and intelligent spraying equipment. The pesticide tax supports the fact that optimising pesticide consumption is always worthwhile. As regards making mandatory the guidelines for IPM, that is integrated pest management, which we refer to as integrated plant protection, I can say that it is already mandatory to cultivate the eight IPM principles that are part of the framework directive on the sustainable use of pesticides. The general principles are implemented through the establishment of a number of concrete actions. Since farms are different, the measures can be very different on the individual farms. More efforts are being made in the current spray strategy to ensure the implementation of the IPM: Targeted advice has been carried out, a number of crop-specific cultivation guidelines have been prepared, and a greater focus on IPM has been given to the training of sprayers. Finally, farmers are informed of the principles at the time of verification. I therefore consider that farmers have a good starting point for implementing the IPM principles and do not believe that a certification scheme for production should be introduced. The overall aim of the pesticide strategy is to reduce the pesticide load by 40%. in 2015 in relation to the calculated level in 2011 based on quantities sold. The load indicators express the potential overall burden on health and the environment from a number of data on the environmental and health properties of pesticides. The latest available data from 2014 show that the load based on sales volumes has decreased by 55%, while the load based on actual consumption has decreased by 18%. in the period 2011-2014. The large difference is probably an indication that, before the pesticide tax came into force, there was considerable hoarding of pesticides and that, at least in 2014, stocks had not yet been used up. The frequency of treatment describes how many times, on average, the total agricultural area could be treated with the pesticides if they were applied in a standard dosage. Despite the historically low sales, the treatment rate in 2014 was 2.7. However, the overall burden of several treatments is less and this is not reflected in the treatment frequency. Therefore, it does not make sense to focus on whether the treatment frequency increases as long as the load indicator falls. Finally, it proposes to strengthen the enforcement of limit values for pesticide residues in food so that penalties are imposed for any overrun. I do not think that is the way forward. It is not true that today there is a lax practice for the enforcement of limit values, because the Danish Food Authority must respond at all times to a violation of the pesticide rules and apply the penalty necessary to motivate compliance with the rules. It would be an aggravating circumstance if there were repeated or serious infringements. However, the penalty must not be unduly intrusive, as there must be proportionality between infringement and sanction. The Danish Food Safety Authority's principles of control focus on the preventive element. Automatic sanctioning at any exceedance could stand in the way of the Authority's ability to use guidance and dialogue. Counselling and dialogue are often the best instruments for companies to comply with the rules in the future. In fact, guidance and dialogue can lead to companies imposing the necessary requirements on their food suppliers, thereby having a preventive effect in relation to exceeding pesticide residue limits. Against this background, the government rejects the overall motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426202711	The motion for a resolution we are debating here will require the government to implement five measures aimed at creating a more sustainable use of pesticides for the benefit of our environment and for the benefit of the health of the Danes. In concrete terms, the proposal involves firstly that pesticides with unintentionally hazardous properties should not be approved in Denmark. Secondly, requirements must be put in place to ensure that the best injection technique is used. In addition, the proposers want a timetable for banning ineffective spraying techniques. Thirdly, the government must ensure that the guidelines currently in place for integrated plant protection are repealed into law and thus become mandatory. Fourthly, action must be taken in cases where the frequency of treatment, i.e. the overall pesticide pressure, increases, even if the load indicators, which can be translated into the harmful effects we know today, fall. Fifthly, the proposers would like to see the limit values for pesticide residues in food be better enforced and any overrun sanctioned. In this context, the Socialists would like to call on the government to call for negotiations on a new pesticide strategy. It is our hope that the government will help to reach a broad agreement that can ensure a sound use of pesticides, where we do not gamble with the health of the Danes or with our environment and nature.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426205346	It is, of course, an important debate that SF is raising today, because the amount of pesticides and pesticides we use in Denmark is of course crucial to how our groundwater is doing. And I think there are many of us who are happy that Denmark is a country where you can tap water from the tap and that it has not been through all possible processing, chlorine treatment and what else you know from other countries. We will look forward to that very much, and we hope that we will be able to take part in that work. We have three basic principles on which we work in the Radical Left. The first is that we put health and water first. So if there is uncertainty as to whether or not a spray has a bad effect, we will be on the side of health and water. We want to keep pushing agriculture towards using fewer pesticides that damage our groundwater. And then we want a transition to more organic farming, because it also reduces the emission of harmful substances. We think that a great many of the SF's proposals in this resolution are excellent. Then you can discuss what it is like to be able to implement EU legislation and some of what some colleagues have said. However, the Radical Left hopes that in committee we may be able to land some report, some text in which we agree to work with each other towards drawing up a strategy for the next three years, which is also what the Minister has proposed and in which we then try to lay down some principles that we want to protect groundwater and some of the things I mentioned just now. So I hope that I can get out of it and we think the SF's proposal sounds reasonable.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426210742	The debate is open. Minister for the Environment and Food.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426210750	Thank you for that, President. And thank you to Enhedslisten for tabling a motion for a resolution with the opportunity to discuss the development of ecology in Denmark. I must say at the outset that there can be no doubt that this government wants agriculture. That is why we have already reached a good and comprehensive agreement on a food and agricultural package. The initiatives in the food and agriculture package together contribute just over 1 billion. kr. in structural GDP and a boost in environmental status in 2021. Of course, that agreement also includes several initiatives to develop a competitive and market-driven ecology sector. Compared to the resolution itself: The Enhedslisten proposes that the Danish Parliament instruct the government to maintain the ecological objective of doubling the ecological area by 2020 compared to 2007. The government cannot support this, and there are several reasons for this. The government does not believe that it is the way forward to re-establish a quantitative target for the organic area. That is why we announced in October last year that we would no longer maintain the quantitative objective. Instead, we believe that the ecological sector in the long run is best served by being market driven. Therefore, a quantitative objective does not make sense. We will have the ecological area that the market requires. Conversion to organic production is a process that typically takes 2 years before you are fully in business and can start marketing organic products. That is why organic farming must be developed through real initiatives which strengthen the individual farmer's belief that organic products will also be marketed in the years to come. Together with our partners in the food and agriculture package, we strengthen the competitiveness of ecology by removing barriers to the whole market chain and focusing on fair production conditions, new research and increased sales and exports. Specifically, we are allocating 90 million. kr. during the period 2016-2018 to help farmers who think about converting their production into ecology, to have a reconversion check of their holdings and to strengthen the continued market security by promoting domestic sales and exports. In addition, in the period 2017-2020, we have allocated more than 1 billion. kr. for organic area subsidies and conversion grants. It could finance approximately 218,000 hectares, and if there were to be a larger search, we have agreed to prioritize additional funds corresponding to Aarhus University's so-called optimistic estimate of the development of the ecological area of 234,500 hectares. Thus, the government has assigned funding to a larger ecological area than ever before. To strengthen the organic sector in the slightly longer term, we are setting up an ecological business team with resources from the industry, which will make concrete recommendations before the end of this year on how competitive and market-driven organic growth can be supported in the areas of marketing, exports, raw materials and innovation. It will therefore be a work that takes place in 2016, but continues into the first quarter of 2017. In addition, we carry out a neighboring check on how ecological rules have been implemented in other EU countries. Among other things: our organic fruit and berry production is poor and does not develop in line with market demand. For example: Danish organic apples are sold out in just a few weeks, which is why we are looking at the framework conditions in the other countries. Several important productions, such as: fruit and berries, greenhouse vegetables and beekeeping do not take up much area compared with e.g. organic milk production, where there must be large grasslands for the animals. Therefore, with a single objective of a given number of hectares, there is a risk of not supporting or, at worst, giving priority to important organic productions, which are not very land-intensive, and the government therefore rejects the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426221011	Thank you. There is a massive increase in the number of farmers applying to be allowed to receive ecological subsidies so that they can get on with the restructuring. It is a branch of activity which for years has had very, very difficult to make money. There are red numbers on the bottom line for a great many producers, and production is associated with a large number of problems that throw really high costs onto the rest of society, including the costs associated with the increasing number of antibiotic-resistant bacteria that are infecting people; also people who have never been in a pig stall. It is also the pollution from the piggy that pollutes watercourses, nature and the marine environment. At the same time, an ambitious ecological target has been dropped, despite the fact that this is where we find the best economy, despite the fact that it is here that demand, both nationally and internationally, is in very, very strong growth. There is therefore no doubt at all as to which branch of production the government is most committed to helping and developing. It is a pig production which does not earn money and whose jobs are increasingly disappearing from the country. That is because it is better to send live pigs south of the border where wages are lower. It is also a production that is very difficult to reconcile with the high environmental requirements that we have to have in Denmark because, as I mentioned, both our nature and our aquatic environment are more vulnerable than in other countries. It has therefore been the high Danish ecological targets that have helped to ensure that we in Denmark are much more advanced on this agenda than in other countries, and that we also manage to increase exports of organic products year after year. ParlaMint-DK_20160426221657	As no one else has asked to speak, the debate is closed. I propose that the proposal be referred to the Committee on the Environment and Food. Are there any comments? It's agreed.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426221708	The debate is open. Minister for the Environment and Food.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426221726	Thank you for that, President, and thank you to the Enhedslisten for highlighting microplastics with this resolution. The presence of microplastics in the environment is a problem we cannot ignore. This has recently been referred to as a major environmental problem, among other things. in the form of wear from car tyres, paint, ship paint and road stripes. And that may be for good reason. The first part of the proposal concerns the introduction of a ban on products that have been added to microplastics, what is called primary microplastics. As far as I understand the proposal, it is mainly the use in care products and the use in artificial lawns that the proposer is aiming at. This type of microplastic is about 11 per cent. of the total releases to the environment of microplastics, but it is less than 1%. of what ends up in the aquatic environment. The use of microplastics in care products contributes little to discharge to the aquatic environment. Our calculations show that it is down to 0.1%, and the cosmetics industry is already phasing out its use in cosmetics throughout the EU. Having said that, I agree with the proposer that microplastics should not be used where there is no good reason for doing so. That is why I have just asked the Commission to draw up a proposal for a ban on microplastics in cosmetic products. The vast majority of products that can be purchased in Denmark are produced outside the country's borders, and therefore an effort aimed at products containing or capable of releasing microplastics should be made at EU level. Of the 11% that primary microplastics make up of the total release to the environment, rubber granules are the largest source. It comes, among other things. from artificial grass fields, where the granules from the track go out to the adjacent areas. Here it can pose a local environmental problem, while only a small part is released to the aquatic environment. The rubber granules are made from discarded car tyres, and therefore the use for artificial grass lawns and the like reduces the amount of wasted tyres that would otherwise have to be deposited or burned. The government is taking care of the problem of rubber granules. We are in the process of preparing a guide on how to handle artificial lawns from being built until they turn into garbage. The problem of limiting the spread of microplastics is covered by this guide. With regard to the second part of the proposal, which aims to prevent the proliferation of secondary microplastics, i.e. microplastics produced by wearing out of products, we are also in the process of doing so. Secondary microplastics account for 89 per cent. of the microplastic released to the environment, but probably more than 99%. of the microplastics entering the aquatic environment from land. We have launched initiatives to find out how microplastics find their way to the aquatic environment and how it can be cleaned most effectively. This concerns, among other things: examining which existing cleaning methods best clean up microplastics and also attempts to develop new technologies to improve cleaning. In addition, a study has been launched on the amount of microplastics added to purification plants, the type and origin of plastics. The study also looks at what happens to microplastics in the treatment plant and where it gets off if it spreads with the sludge on the farmland. Before we have a thorough knowledge of where the microplastic comes from and where it goes, I think it is too early to think in a general strategy for limiting the spread of microplastics. We also do not know which methods are best at measuring microplastics in water, so for that reason alone it is too early to plan a possible measuring programme for microplastics in effluents from wastewater treatment plants. The spread of microplastics in the aquatic environment is a cross-border problem which cannot be solved in Denmark alone. Probably large parts of the microplastic that we find in the oceans stem from the degradation of larger pieces of plastic waste that is scattered in the oceans. Therefore, Denmark works with neighbouring countries, among others. Within the framework of the regional marine conventions for the North Sea, the Atlantic and the Baltic Sea to limit marine waste. The European Commission has announced that as part of their action plan for the circular economy, they will draw up a plastic strategy in 2017. The strategy must take into account the problems caused by plastics throughout the life cycle of the plastic. From the Danish side, of course, we will contribute to the development of the strategy, and we are working to ensure that the strategy includes initiatives to reduce pollution by microplastics. In this context, it might be obvious to discuss whether standards for the release of microplastics from products or materials could be developed. There is no point in national initiatives here, because most products are produced for the European market or for the global market. I assume that we will go into this in greater depth when we deal with motion for a resolution B-94 on effective action against plastic pollution, which is precisely about preventing the spread of plastic and hence microplastics in nature. So we are already well in the process of making sensible efforts in those areas where we know enough to act now and, for example, in those areas where we know enough to act now. use of microplastics in cosmetics, we do that. In areas where a great deal of knowledge is still lacking, we are launching studies and technology development. I think it is important to take action at the right level, and when it comes to cross-border issues and restricting the opportunities for the sale of products, that is, within the framework of the European Union. That is why the government cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426225022	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it is the rapporteur for the proposers, Mrs Reumert Gjerding.
ParlaMint-DK_20160426225045	I am very pleased with the debate we have had here today, because I think it shows that, across Parliament as a whole, we agree that we are faced with a relatively new environmental problem which we are all taking very seriously, and we agree that something needs to be done about it. What we may disagree on is the pace, but then we have something to discuss and keep sticking to in the future. If you have been dealing with the environment for many years, you know that new environmental problems are developing gradually. And there is no doubt that plastic pollution and micro-plastic pollution are one of the major environmental problems we are facing now, both in relation to this huge plastic soup or the huge plastic islands, or whatever we are going to call those that are floating around in the oceans of the world, on many, many hundreds of tonnes, and which I will look forward to seeing the committee going out to see, so that we can see for ourselves, but also in relation to the increasing amount of micro-plastics which are being absorbed into the food chain and which can carry dangerous chemicals and which are ultimately in danger of ending up at the top of the food chain, which is in our own bodies. So I would say that there is a connection between these large plastic islands and the microplastic, because the plastic is gradually breaking down. So the plastic contamination with large plastic pieces, which is probably in the Pacific Ocean and elsewhere, also turns into microplastics at some point. The proposal here, tabled by Enhedslisten, has been further updated by a study published last week by DTU Aqua, where 400 herring and cod were examined and where biologists found small plastic fibres in every five fish. That means that this is a very serious problem, also because it is the case that if we first get it in our own bodies, it is very difficult to separate. This means that it accumulates in the body, and it is suspected of causing cell death, infections or other types of immune-intensive conditions. So, both for the sake of the environment and indeed for the sake of health, this is an area of priority. This is an area that we also need to focus on here in the Folketing in relation to regularly discussing what we can do with it and what we can do on the Danish side. And in this context, I think it is very positive that the Minister for the Environment has announced that, as such, we are already in the process. As I also mentioned in my question to the Minister, it is not so long ago that the Danish Parliament has been given an answer that Denmark was not among the countries that addressed this issue in the European Union, and that is now, and I think that is very positive. You can do different things. You can both and maybe it is the most obvious thing to start by doing • removing micro pearls where they are added, for example. in cosmetic products and where it is not necessary at all. It's a place you can put in right now. The second and more complex, which, as the Minister also mentioned, is what the statistics take up the most, is all that comes from wear and tear, for example. of artificial grass or shoe soles or car tires and other things, which are more difficult to overcome, because one cannot replace that type of use from one day to the next with something else. And that is also why we need to have a more open approach to it and say that we need to learn more about what the sources are, what the substitutes are for the various sources, and how we can be better at cleaning the purification plants so that we can remove microplastics and avoid it ending up in the environment. Overall, I sensed a very positive approach to the resolution. I look forward to the committee's work becoming even more clever in terms of global plastic pollution and its consequences, and we shall have to discuss, as has also been mentioned, an even more far-reaching proposal before long, and I think it is really good that we in the Danish Parliament regularly discuss one of the most important environmental problems of our time at all. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160426225532	I'm sorry I missed the first round. So you must have me forgiven. But, of course, I have heard the debate, and I think it is a sympathetic proposal again. It is often when it comes from the Enhedslisten. But there are great practical problems. It would be enormously comprehensive to ban products with microplastics, because there are so many products that contain it. This is something that should be resolved at European level, because a purely Danish ban would be incredibly difficult to enforce. That is why we are pleased that some initiatives are being taken and we would also very much like to talk about it. I can assure you that this is a problem which we also take very seriously. We think it is one of the world's major environmental problems, and it is very good that there is something more focus on it in those years, and that is something that should be seen here in the light of. So we look forward to discussing it further, but we do not think we can vote through a purely Danish ban.
